**Assistant — Turn 12**

# *Nisemonogatari* deep dive — fakes, justice, family, and the ethics of choosing the false

After *Bakemonogatari* and *Kizumonogatari*, 『偽物語』 initially feels like a tonal swerve: more domestic comedy, more siblings, more fanservice provocation, more absurd conversations, and less of the tight tragic gravity that organized *Kizu*. But structurally it is doing something very important.

If *Bakemonogatari* asks:

> What pain has this person displaced into an oddity?

And *Kizumonogatari* asks:

> What wound created Araragi’s compulsion to save?

Then *Nisemonogatari* asks:

> What happens when the ideals people live by are fake — and they choose them anyway?

This is why the title matters. 偽物語 is not merely “Impostor Story” because fake specialists, fake curses, and fake sisters appear. It is about the unsettling possibility that **fakeness is not the opposite of value**.

By the end, the text has moved toward one of the series’ most important propositions:

> A fake may be morally inferior to the real.
> A fake may also be more intense than the real, because it has to try to become what the real simply is.

That idea will matter enormously later for Kaiki, for Araragi, for Shinobu, for the Fire Sisters, and for the series’ larger treatment of identity.

---

## 1\. The place of *Nisemonogatari* in the early series

The first three works form a very clear progression:

**『化物語』** — oddities as displaced adolescent wounds.
**『傷物語』** — the origin wound beneath Araragi’s present life.
**『偽物語』** — the problem of fake ideals, fake justice, fake family certainty, and fake humanity.

In *Bake*, Araragi encounters girls whose internal contradictions have become怪異. In *Kizu*, Araragi himself becomes the contradiction. In *Nise*, the story moves from the wounded individual to the **social and ethical masks** people use to move through the world.

The central terms shift:

-   from **怪異 / oddity**

-   to **傷 / wound**

-   to **偽物 / fake**

-   and, importantly, **正義 / justice**


That last term dominates *Nise*. The Fire Sisters call themselves justice. Araragi calls them fake justice. Kaiki weaponizes fraudulent evil. Kagenui represents professional, punitive justice. Araragi rejects all of those clean labels and chooses family attachment even if it makes him a hypocrite.

So *Nisemonogatari* is not just “the sister arc.” It is the first major book where the series stops asking only “how do we save someone?” and begins asking:

> What if the thing someone needs saving from is an ideal?

---

## 2\. The two-part structure: Karen Bee and Tsukihi Phoenix

The split between the two volumes is elegant.

### Karen Bee

**Karen Bee** is about fake justice.

Karen and Tsukihi, the “Fire Sisters,” are famous middle-school vigilantes. Karen is the action half; Tsukihi is the strategist. They interfere in local disputes, punish wrongdoers, and treat themselves as a kind of grassroots righteous force.

Araragi looks at them and says: this is childish. This is not justice. This is **正義の味方ごっこ** — playing at being allies of justice.

But he is not neutral. He is their brother. His contempt is tangled with pride, embarrassment, inferiority, protectiveness, and resentment. He sees their justice as fake partly because it is fake, but also because they remind him of his own fake heroism.

The antagonist, Kaiki Deishuu, is a fake specialist, a fake exorcist, a fake authority — and yet he is also one of the most dangerous truth-tellers in the series. He scams middle schoolers with curses and charms, but his fakery produces real consequences. Karen’s bee condition is therefore the perfect incident: a fake oddity, or a quasi-fake oddity, creates a real fever, real danger, real moral panic.

### Tsukihi Phoenix

**Tsukihi Phoenix** is about fake identity.

Tsukihi is revealed to be not simply Araragi’s human sister, but an oddity: the **しでの鳥 / dying bird**, a phoenix-like immortal being that replaces or inhabits a human child. Kagenui and Ononoki come to exterminate her because, from their specialist perspective, she is not human. She is a fake sister. A counterfeit human. A monster wearing family form.

Araragi’s answer is blunt: fake or not, she is his sister.

This is the thematic climax of *Nise*. In *Karen Bee*, Araragi criticizes fake justice. In *Tsukihi Phoenix*, he chooses fake family if the alternative is “true” justice that kills the person he loves.

So the two arcs mirror each other:

That is a major step beyond *Bakemonogatari*. The question is no longer simply whether pain is real. It is whether **a false thing can become worth protecting**.

---

## 3\. “偽物” as the book’s central category

The book’s key idea is not “fake bad, real good.” That would be far too simple for Nisio Isin.

Instead, *Nisemonogatari* gives us several competing theories of the fake.

### Araragi’s early theory

Araragi begins from a fairly ordinary position:

> Real justice matters.
> Fake justice is immature, dangerous, and embarrassing.

That is why he calls the Fire Sisters fake. They are not law. They are not mature moral agents. They do not understand consequences. They are energetic middle-schoolers acting out a heroic fantasy.

But Araragi’s criticism is unstable because he himself is not exactly “real” either. He is a quasi-vampire pretending to be a normal human. He is a fake ordinary student. A fake survivor. A fake hero. A fake older brother who failed to keep his sisters away from danger. His authority to condemn fakery is compromised.

### Shinobu’s theory

Shinobu’s comment that a fake can be “more real than the real” because it imitates the real sharply reframes the whole book. This is the first clear articulation of a central *Monogatari* paradox:

> Fakes have form.
> Fakes have intention.
> Fakes may struggle toward the real more consciously than the real does.

That does not make every fake noble. Kaiki’s scams are still scams. But the statement opens the door to a more complicated moral universe.

### Kaiki’s theory

The most famous version comes through Kaiki, later reported by Kagenui: the fake has greater value because it has the will to become real. The real simply exists. The fake performs, strives, imitates, constructs, deceives, and therefore reveals desire.

Kaiki is a fraud, but the line is not fraudulent. That is the disturbing thing about him. He is a liar who sometimes tells the truth more clearly than the honest characters can.

### Kagenui’s theory

Kagenui initially takes the opposite position. If something is fake, it is to be distinguished, judged, and removed. The fake human must be treated as an oddity. Tsukihi’s lived family role does not matter enough to override ontological category.

This is specialist ethics hardened into extermination.

### Araragi’s final theory

Araragi’s final position is not philosophical purity. It is choice.

> If loving Tsukihi is fake, then he will be fake.
> If protecting her is hypocrisy, then he will be a hypocrite.
> If his family love is legally, metaphysically, or morally invalid, he accepts the stain.

This is not a rational refutation of Kagenui. It is a declaration of partiality.

That is why *Nise* is important. Araragi moves from wanting to save people in general to admitting that he will choose his people in particular.

That is morally messier — and more human.

---

## 4\. Karen Araragi: fake justice as bodily sincerity

Karen is initially presented as absurd: tall, athletic, reckless, violently energetic, easily baited, and attached to a juvenile righteousness that Araragi finds ridiculous.

But she is not shallow.

Karen’s justice is fake because it is conceptually immature. She sees injustice, charges forward, and tries to punch the problem. She does not understand social systems, deception, adult malice, legal procedure, or the way “saving one person” may create new harm elsewhere. In that sense, Araragi’s criticism is justified.

But Karen’s justice is also sincere in the body.

That is the important distinction. Karen may be intellectually childish, but she physically means what she says. She throws herself into situations. She endures fever. She refuses to give in. She fights Araragi despite being ill. She cuts her own hair without hesitation when the absurd logic of a challenge demands it.

Karen is fake justice, but she is not fake passion.

Her body is where her sincerity resides. She does not theorize justice; she metabolizes it as motion. This makes her a useful contrast to Araragi. Araragi also throws his body into danger, but he is more guilt-driven and inwardly twisted. Karen’s self-endangerment is more straightforward, almost animal.

Her flaw is not cowardice or hypocrisy. Her flaw is **unexamined righteousness**.

She wants to be on the side of justice before she understands what justice costs.

---

## 5\. Tsukihi Araragi: fake justice as temperament, fake humanity as family

Tsukihi is much stranger than Karen even before the phoenix reveal.

Where Karen is direct, Tsukihi is volatile, clever, theatrical, and emotionally dangerous. Karen’s justice is action. Tsukihi’s justice is style, arrangement, manipulation, and mood.

Araragi’s distinction between them is very sharp: Karen tends toward acting for purposes; Tsukihi tends toward making purposes serve her methods. Karen is “justice” as forward motion. Tsukihi is “justice” as hobby, performance, and taste.

That sounds damning, and it partly is. But the phoenix reveal turns the judgment inside out.

Tsukihi is literally fake. The girl Araragi knows is, in some metaphysical sense, not the original human child. Yet from the family’s perspective, that distinction is emotionally meaningless. The being called Tsukihi has lived as their daughter, their sister, their noisy household disaster. She has been loved, tolerated, fought with, protected, and remembered as Tsukihi.

Here *Nisemonogatari* makes its most radical move:

> Identity is not only origin.
> Identity is also relation, duration, role, memory, and attachment.

Kagenui’s category may be correct: Tsukihi is an oddity. But Araragi’s category is also correct: Tsukihi is his sister.

The book refuses to let ontology settle ethics.

That is important for the whole series. *Monogatari* repeatedly asks whether people are defined by what they are, what they do, what they remember, what others call them, or what they choose to become. Tsukihi’s case pushes that problem into family identity.

---

## 6\. The Fire Sisters: why their fake justice still matters

Karen and Tsukihi are not “real” heroes. Araragi is right about that.

But the series does not simply mock them.

The Fire Sisters are fake because they imitate an ideal they do not fully understand. But imitation is also how people grow into ideals. A child playing hero is not a hero, but the performance is not meaningless. It can train the shape of future courage.

This is why Kaiki’s fake theory matters so much. If a fake contains the will to become real, then the Fire Sisters’ justice is not worthless. It is immature, dangerous, ridiculous — but not empty.

The problem is that the Fire Sisters live in a world where adults like Kaiki exist.

That is the real collision in *Karen Bee*:

> childish justice meets adult fraud.

Karen is not prepared for Kaiki because Kaiki does not fight in the world she understands. He does not need to beat her physically. He uses rumor, money, narrative, suggestion, and social weakness. He is not “strong” in Karen’s terms. He is poisonous in a way her justice cannot grasp.

This is where Araragi’s older-brother role becomes legitimate. He is not morally superior because he is more righteous. He is more experienced with contamination. He has already learned from *Kizu* and *Bake* that problems are rarely solved by pure frontal assault.

Karen has strength. Araragi has scars.

That is why he can intervene.

---

## 7\. Kaiki Deishuu: fake specialist, real nihilist, anti-Oshino

Kaiki is one of the most important additions to the series. He is introduced as a villain, but he is not a conventional villain. He is a con man, a fake specialist, a seller of curses, a manipulator of middle-school social anxiety, and the man who once helped destroy Senjougahara’s family financially and emotionally.

He is contemptible.

But he is also philosophically dangerous because he is not stupid, not theatrical in the expected way, and not invested in the romance of evil.

Kaiki’s evil is banal, commercial, and anti-dramatic.

He does not want cosmic power.
He does not want revenge.
He does not want ideological victory.
He does not want to be recognized as a great villain.
He wants money.

That makes him almost more disturbing than the vampire hunters or oddities. He is not a monster from outside society. He is a parasite inside ordinary society: exploiting grief, gullibility, family desperation, adolescent insecurity, and spiritual hunger.

### Kaiki versus Oshino

Oshino and Kaiki are mirror figures.

Oshino is a real specialist who refuses to say he saves people.

Kaiki is a fake specialist who sometimes produces real effects.

Oshino balances.
Kaiki profits.

Oshino demystifies to reduce harm.
Kaiki mystifies to extract money.

Oshino refuses sentimental rescue.
Kaiki refuses sentimentality altogether.

Yet Kaiki sometimes tells truths Oshino would not say so brutally. His conversation with Senjougahara is especially revealing. He tells her that the man who harmed her died meaninglessly, that her mother will not return in some dramatic reconciliation, and that the past is not building toward a grand confrontation. This is cruel. It is also, in a limited way, liberating.

Kaiki’s worldview is:

> Do not expect life to have dramatic closure.
> Do not expect trauma to return in narratively satisfying form.
> Do not expect villains to become meaningful enough to defeat.

That is morally ugly because he uses this worldview to justify exploitation. But the insight itself has force. He punctures the heroic narrative. He tells Senjougahara that her pain does not guarantee a climactic reckoning.

For a series full of supernatural symbolism, Kaiki introduces a cold anti-symbolic logic:

> Sometimes the past is just over.
> Sometimes the person who hurt you is not waiting to become your final boss.
> Sometimes evil is petty and gone.

That makes him a vital character.

---

## 8\. Senjougahara after Kaiki: recovery without dramatic closure

Senjougahara’s role in *Nise* is smaller than in *Bake*, but thematically crucial.

Her confrontation with Kaiki is one of the first moments where we see her post-crab recovery tested against the source of her old damage. In a simpler story, this would be catharsis: the victim confronts the scammer, denounces him, perhaps defeats him, and gains closure.

*Nisemonogatari* refuses that.

Kaiki does not become grand enough to justify catharsis. He remains petty, slippery, and spiritually ugly. He does not give Senjougahara a clean victory. He tells her things she does not want to hear, and then exits without becoming the dramatic enemy she might have imagined.

That is painful, but it fits the series’ psychology. Senjougahara’s recovery cannot depend on the world arranging her trauma into a satisfying scene. She has already reclaimed her weight. She has already chosen Araragi. She is already living beyond the wound. Kaiki no longer gets to be the center of her story.

This is why his line about “bright Senjougahara” having no value to him matters. He values wounded people because wounded people can be exploited. A healed or healing Senjougahara is less profitable.

That is almost accidentally beautiful:

> Her recovery makes her useless to the man who once used her.

---

## 9\. Shinobu in *Nise*: from silent wound to speaking partner

One of the biggest developments in *Nisemonogatari* is Shinobu’s re-entry into speech.

In *Bake*, Shinobu is largely silent, the living residue of *Kizu*. In *Nise*, she becomes Araragi’s companion again: sarcastic, regal, childish, hungry for donuts, and philosophically lucid.

This matters because the Araragi-Shinobu relationship begins to move from punishment to partnership.

Not full equality — not yet. The wound remains. Araragi still controls the power balance in some ways; Shinobu still depends on him; their past still stains everything. But speech changes the relation. Silence was the form of their bad ending. Conversation becomes the first sign that the bad ending can be lived through.

Shinobu’s ethical self-definition is also important: she will neither destroy humanity nor save it. She explicitly distinguishes herself from Araragi. He helps people in trouble. She does not. Even weakened, she refuses to pretend she is human.

This gives her dignity. She is not simply “becoming nicer.” She is choosing a rule that lets her remain herself without returning to predation.

That is a major evolution from *Kizu*. Kiss-shot was a vampire queen seeking death. Shinobu is a diminished vampire choosing a bounded existence.

---

## 10\. Araragi and Shinobu: shared body, shared guilt, shared action

The climax of *Tsukihi Phoenix* depends on Araragi temporarily restoring Shinobu’s power through blood. This is not just an action upgrade. It is a symbolic reactivation of the *Kizu* bond.

In *Kizu*, blood exchange destroyed Araragi’s humanity and bound him to Kiss-shot.

In *Nise*, blood exchange becomes tactical, chosen, and relational. Araragi and Shinobu use their bond to protect Tsukihi.

That is a major change.

The same mechanism that once produced a bad ending now becomes a way to defend the family Araragi chooses. This does not erase the old wound, but it repurposes it.

Shinobu’s participation is also ethically specific. She is not saving humanity. She is helping Araragi. Her partiality mirrors his. She does not care about Tsukihi as an abstract innocent; she acts because Araragi is her master/partner/host/wound-companion.

This makes *Nise* a book about partial ethics on multiple levels:

-   Araragi protects his sister because she is his sister.

-   Shinobu helps because Araragi is Araragi.

-   Senjougahara acts because Araragi matters to her.

-   Karen and Tsukihi act because their local social world matters to them.

-   Kaiki acts because money matters to him.

-   Kagenui acts because her specialist code matters to her.


No one is neutral. Everyone’s ethics are situated.

---

## 11\. Kagenui Yozuru: justice as ontological enforcement

Kagenui is a fascinating contrast to both Oshino and Kaiki.

Oshino balances.
Kaiki deceives.
Kagenui judges.

She is a specialist, but her specialty is not gentle mediation. She deals with immortal oddities and treats them as violations. Tsukihi, from her perspective, is an abomination: a fake human, a counterfeit child, a being that replaces and continues without legitimate origin.

Kagenui is not stupid or evil. That makes her more interesting. Her position is internally coherent. If oddities can impersonate humans, then specialists must distinguish and eliminate them. If humanity matters as a category, then counterfeit humanity is a threat.

But *Nise* pushes against her certainty.

Her justice is real in a way the Fire Sisters’ justice is not. She has knowledge, discipline, power, and professional clarity. Yet the book makes her real justice look frighteningly inhuman. She is correct about Tsukihi’s nature, but her correctness does not exhaust Tsukihi’s meaning.

This is one of the series’ strongest ethical moves:

> Accurate classification can still produce a morally wrong action.

Kagenui knows what Tsukihi is. Araragi knows who Tsukihi is.

The fight is between those two forms of knowledge.

---

## 12\. Ononoki Yotsugi: the artificial girl as deadpan mirror

Ononoki’s role is quieter here, but important.

She is herself an artificial or constructed being, a corpse-like familiar/doll figure, which makes her presence in the Tsukihi arc thematically pointed. She stands beside Kagenui as a weaponized fake person while they prepare to kill another fake person.

Her deadpan speech, formulaic catchphrases, and doll-like presentation make her an embodied question:

> If artificiality disqualifies Tsukihi, why does Ononoki’s artificiality function as a tool of justice?

The text does not fully explore that yet, but the seed is planted. Ononoki is one more proof that in *Monogatari*, the fake is not outside the moral field. It moves, speaks, judges, fights, and may eventually become a person through relation.

That will matter later.

---

## 13\. Araragi’s older-brother ethics

*Nisemonogatari* is the first book where Araragi’s family role becomes central.

In *Bake*, family is mostly other people’s wound: Senjougahara’s mother/father, Hachikuji’s mother, Hanekawa’s household. In *Kizu*, Araragi’s family is mostly background, the normal life he risks losing. In *Nise*, the Araragi household becomes the battlefield.

This changes Araragi’s ethics.

He is no longer helping strangers, classmates, or ambiguous love interests. He is dealing with his sisters — people he finds annoying, embarrassing, ridiculous, and deeply precious.

That is important because sibling love is not idealized here. Araragi is often irritated by Karen and Tsukihi. He mocks them, fights them, underestimates them, and calls their justice fake. But when the threat becomes real, his attachment is non-negotiable.

This is not the same as universal compassion. It is family partiality.

His final defense of Tsukihi is not:

> She is innocent.
> She is human.
> She deserves protection under a universal principle.

It is closer to:

> She is my sister.
> I do not care if this is fake.
> I choose her.

That is morally dangerous if universalized. Plenty of evil begins from protecting “one’s own” against all accountability. But in this specific case, Araragi’s partiality corrects Kagenui’s cold classification.

The book does not solve the tension. It stages it.

---

## 14\. Justice, evil, and the collapse of clean opposition

The opening of *Tsukihi Phoenix* is unusually explicit about justice and evil. Araragi reflects that justice defeating evil does not end evil. New evil emerges; sometimes new evil was once justice. Justice is not a stable eternal category. It is historically replaceable, socially reversible, and often dependent on having an enemy.

This is much more politically and ethically mature than the Fire Sisters’ worldview.

The Fire Sisters imagine justice as action against bad people.

Araragi, after *Kizu* and *Bake*, knows that:

-   bad people have families;

-   victims can become perpetrators;

-   good intentions can harm;

-   monsters can be pitiable;

-   humans can be monstrous;

-   specialists can be cruel;

-   fake things can have value;

-   real things can be morally empty.


This is why he can no longer believe in simple justice. But he also cannot retreat into Kaiki’s nihilism.

That is the philosophical tension of *Nise*:

> Fire Sisters: justice is obvious.
> Kaiki: justice is useful only as a scam target.
> Kagenui: justice is professional extermination of the false.
> Araragi: justice is compromised, but attachment still demands action.

Araragi’s position is not clean, but it is alive.

---

## 15\. Fanservice, incest comedy, and the problem of provocation

A rigorous analysis has to acknowledge that *Nisemonogatari* is the most aggressively provocative early volume in terms of sexualized comedy, especially involving the sisters.

The toothbrush scene is infamous for a reason. It is funny, uncomfortable, excessive, and deliberately staged as a parody of erotic escalation. It is also the kind of scene where “the text is self-aware” does not fully neutralize the discomfort.

The best way to understand it literarily is that *Nise* is obsessed with the boundaries between categories:

-   sibling / erotic object

-   human / oddity

-   fake / real

-   justice / hobby

-   specialist / fraud

-   care / control

-   comedy / violation


The sister fanservice is part of that boundary-testing. It turns domestic intimacy grotesquely theatrical. It asks how far performance, teasing, and genre convention can go before the category “family” becomes unstable.

But this does not mean every instance is defensible. As with the Hachikuji and Nadeko material in *Bake*, the work’s brilliance coexists with material that is morally and aesthetically risky. Sometimes the provocation sharpens the theme. Sometimes it feels indulgent. Often it is both.

For this project, I would frame it this way:

> *Nisemonogatari* deliberately contaminates family comedy with erotic performance because the book is about fake categories and unstable roles. But the degree of sexualized sister comedy also means the text participates in the very otaku-market provocations it is partly parodying.

That tension is part of the work, not something to pretend away.

---

## 16\. How *Nise* revises Araragi

After *Kizu*, Araragi is a wounded rescuer.

After *Bake*, he is a compulsive helper learning that people must participate in their own recovery.

After *Nise*, he becomes something more specific:

> a person willing to be morally impure for the sake of chosen bonds.

This is a major development.

In *Kizu*, he refuses to kill Kiss-shot even though killing her would restore him.

In *Bake*, he helps girls afflicted by oddities but gradually learns the limits of saving.

In *Nise*, he openly accepts the label of fake, hypocrite, or even bad person if that is the price of protecting Tsukihi.

This is not moral decline. It is moral thickening.

Araragi is less innocent now. He no longer imagines that helping can be pure. His love for his sister is not a universal ethic. His protection of Tsukihi is not clean justice. It is family, attachment, selfishness, loyalty, and love fused together.

That makes him more adult in one way and more dangerous in another.

He has learned that good action may require accepting impurity.

---

## 17\. How *Nise* reframes the earlier heroines

Even though this is the sister arc, it also quietly repositions the earlier cast.

### Senjougahara

She is no longer merely the girl saved from the crab. She is someone who has grown beyond Kaiki’s usefulness. Her relationship with Araragi also becomes more openly intimate and strategic; she is willing to restrain him, protect him, and act without indulging his hero complex.

### Hanekawa

Her brief role reminds us that she remains the “real” standard in Araragi’s mind. He still thinks of her as almost without fake elements, which is precisely the dangerous idealization that later works will continue to dismantle.

### Kanbaru

She becomes part of the comic-social network around Araragi, but her presence also reinforces how bodily desire, performance, and roleplay remain unstable in this series.

### Hachikuji

Her conversation with Araragi about secrets and family is more important than it first appears. She tells him that sharing a secret may relieve the speaker while burdening the listener. That is an unusually mature response, especially from a character framed through childish comedy. It also directly connects to the family theme: love does not require total disclosure.

### Shinobu

She undergoes the most significant shift. She becomes a speaking ethical agent again, not just the silent residue of *Kizu*.

---

## 18\. *Nise* as a bridge toward later *Monogatari*

*Nisemonogatari* is sometimes treated as less “serious” than *Bake* or *Kizu*, but thematically it is indispensable.

It introduces or develops several long-term pillars:

1.  **Kaiki**, who will become one of the series’ most important moral counterweights.

2.  **The fake/real problem**, which later becomes central to identity, performance, and narration.

3.  **The Araragi-Shinobu partnership**, which begins to move beyond silent guilt.

4.  **Specialists as ideologically diverse**, not just Oshino-like balancers.

5.  **Family as chosen relation rather than biological certainty.**

6.  **Araragi’s willingness to accept moral impurity.**

7.  **The idea that truth is not always sufficient for ethical judgment.**


That last one is huge. Kagenui knows the truth about Tsukihi. But truth does not settle the matter.

This is a major evolution from *Bakemonogatari*, where naming the oddity and naming the buried feeling are often crucial to resolution. *Nise* says: sometimes even when the truth is known, the ethical question remains.

---

# Final thesis

*Nisemonogatari* is a book about fake things that cannot be dismissed.

The Fire Sisters’ justice is fake, but their courage is real.
Kaiki’s expertise is fake, but his words sometimes cut into truth.
Araragi’s humanity is fake, but his attachments are real.
Tsukihi’s human origin is fake, but her life as Araragi’s sister is real.
Shinobu’s current form is fake compared to Kiss-shot’s original grandeur, but her bond with Araragi is becoming real.
Even justice itself may be fake if it cannot survive contact with love, family, and consequence.

The core movement from *Bake* to *Kizu* to *Nise* now looks like this:

> **『化物語』:** pain becomes oddity.
> **『傷物語』:** rescue becomes wound.
> **『偽物語』:** fakeness becomes relation.

A concise formulation:

> **『偽物語』 argues that authenticity is not merely origin, purity, or correctness. Authenticity can also be produced by persistence, imitation, attachment, and the willingness to bear the consequences of choosing what others call false.**

That is why Tsukihi’s arc is the true climax. Araragi does not prove she is human. He does not need to.

He chooses the fake — and by choosing it, makes it part of his real life.

**Assistant — Turn 14**

# *Nekomonogatari Black* deep dive — Hanekawa, family, stress, and the ethics of not solving the problem

After reading 『猫物語（黒）』, I would frame it as the **true Hanekawa foundation text**.

*Bakemonogatari*’s 「つばさキャット」 shows Hanekawa breaking down after Araragi begins moving toward Senjougahara. *Kizumonogatari* shows why Araragi idealizes Hanekawa so intensely: she was the first person who reached him after his anti-friendship isolation and vampiric catastrophe. *Nekomonogatari Black* fills in the missing middle: the Golden Week incident where Araragi first discovers that Hanekawa’s “perfection” is not a virtue in the simple sense, but a dangerous survival system.

The title of the chapter is **第禁話　つばさファミリー** — not simply “Tsubasa Cat,” but “Tsubasa Family,” and even the numbering is strange: **禁話**, a forbidden story. That is exactly right. This is the story the earlier books circle around but cannot comfortably tell: the story of Hanekawa’s family, or more precisely, the story of how she has been forced to live without a family while still behaving as if nothing is wrong.

My working thesis:

> **『猫物語（黒）』 is about a girl who has become so good at being “correct” that her suffering can only survive by becoming someone else.**

Black Hanekawa is not merely Hanekawa’s “dark side.” That is too crude. The sawarineko is closer to an emergency outlet, a stress-disposal system, a monstrous helper that appears because Hanekawa herself has made ordinary complaint, anger, self-pity, hatred, and need almost impossible.

The problem is not that Hanekawa knows too little.

The problem is that she knows too much and still refuses to let knowledge become self-protection.

---

## 1\. Where *Neko Black* fits in the early series

The early sequence now has a very clear architecture:

That last word matters: **environment**.

The afterword explicitly circles the idea that not every problem in life is cleanly “solved.” People often adapt to problems, absorb them, live around them, and treat them as part of the surrounding world. That is the philosophy of this book. *Neko Black* is not a story where the family problem is solved. It is a story where the family problem is briefly converted into a supernatural disaster, suppressed, and then left waiting.

The ending says this almost directly: the characters **先送りした** the problem — postponed it.

That makes *Neko Black* one of the most honest early *Monogatari* texts. It knows its own resolution is not a resolution.

---

## 2\. Hanekawa Tsubasa: not “perfect,” but over-adapted

The opening description of Hanekawa is very telling. Araragi lists her readable attributes: class president, honor student, glasses, braids, serious, kind, intelligent, fair. Then he immediately says those “character setting” labels cannot explain her.

This is one of Nisio Isin’s cleanest attacks on archetype-reading. Hanekawa looks like a familiar type: the perfect委員長 / class president. But the novel’s whole point is that the type is false not because Hanekawa is secretly evil, but because the type is too small for her contradiction.

Hanekawa is not faking intelligence.
She is not faking kindness.
She is not faking competence.
She really is extraordinary.

That is what makes her dangerous.

A lesser version of this story would say: “The perfect girl secretly has darkness.” *Neko Black* says something sharper:

> Hanekawa’s problem is not that her goodness is fake.
> Her problem is that her goodness works too well.

She can normalize anything. She can explain anything. She can keep smiling through anything. She can be hit by a parent and describe it with bizarre composure. She can bury a mangled cat corpse with tenderness and no self-dramatization. She can talk about her family structure as if she were summarizing a genealogy chart rather than describing a life almost entirely deprived of belonging.

That makes her “strength” frightening.

She does not collapse where a normal person would collapse. She routes pain into correctness.

---

## 3\. “Family” as absence: why the chapter title hurts

The most important revelation is not simply that Hanekawa’s household is abusive. It is that the household is **not really a family system at all**.

Her biological father is uncertain or absent. Her biological mother is dead by suicide. Her later parental figures are connected through remarriage, obligation, convenience, and residual responsibility rather than love. The people she lives with are “father” and “mother” in a legal or household sense, but the emotional reality is much colder.

Araragi’s later exploration of her house is one of the book’s most devastating scenes. He finds evidence that Hanekawa lives there — school things, clothes, bedding, phone charger, bag — but he cannot find the feeling that she belongs there. Her presence is logistical, not domestic. The house has her objects but not her trace.

That distinction is brutal.

It is not that Hanekawa is homeless in the simple physical sense. She has an address. She has guardians. She has a place to sleep. But the novel makes clear that she lacks what a home usually gives a child: a place where suffering is noticed before it becomes catastrophic.

Araragi’s own family scenes are obscene, noisy, violent, comedic, and full of ridiculous sibling intimacy. Tsukihi attacks him with a crowbar; Karen is a storm of physical stupidity; the Araragi house is overfull with life.

That comedy is not random padding. It is the contrast.

Araragi’s family is absurd but warm.
Hanekawa’s household is orderly but empty.

This is why Araragi’s reaction after visiting Hanekawa’s house is so strong. He flees back to his own home and clings to Tsukihi. It is not erotic comedy in that moment. It is panic. He has seen what a house without belonging looks like, and he instinctively grabs the first proof that his own life has family warmth, however stupid and chaotic.

The title **つばさファミリー** is therefore almost cruel. This is Hanekawa’s family story, but the story is about the absence of family.

---

## 4\. The sawarineko: trigger, outlet, accomplice

The sawarineko / 障り猫 is described as a relatively common oddity story: a dead cat is pitied or touched, and then the person who touched it begins behaving violently or strangely. Oshino’s account makes the folkloric moral explicit: the cat is not the true source of evil. It reveals or releases the underside of a supposedly good person.

That is why Hanekawa is the perfect host.

She is “good” in such an extreme way that her underside has nowhere to go.

The cat is not simply possessing her from outside. The climax makes clear that the relation is stranger: the cat has, in a sense, allowed itself to be taken into Hanekawa. It responds to her lack of pity. Hanekawa does not look down on the dead cat as a poor, pitiful thing. She treats it according to rule, ethics, and fact. Just as she treated vampire-damaged Araragi without pity in *Kizu*, she does not reduce the cat to “かわいそう.”

That matters enormously.

Araragi realizes that both he and the cat were drawn to Hanekawa because she did not pity them. She did not look down on the wounded thing. She did not make herself superior by compassion.

This gives the cat more dignity than a simple “evil spirit” reading. It is not merely using Hanekawa. It is trying to help her, in its crude catlike way. It attacks people, drains energy, and creates havoc because it has become the executor of the stress Hanekawa will not admit.

The sawarineko is therefore:

-   a trigger;

-   a parasite;

-   a stress outlet;

-   a distorted protector;

-   an accomplice to Hanekawa’s repression;

-   and, strangely, one more being that has fallen for Hanekawa’s refusal to pity.


That last point is very *Monogatari*. Even the monster has an emotional reason.

---

## 5\. Stress as the true monster

The central word of the book is **ストレス**.

That sounds almost comically mundane compared to vampires, gods, monkeys’ paws, snakes, phoenixes, and specialist weapons. But that is the point. The great horror under *Neko Black* is not an ancient demon. It is accumulated domestic stress.

Hanekawa’s stress is not spectacular. It is daily. It is environmental. It comes from living in a place where she is tolerated but not loved, from being treated as an inconvenience, from having no proper emotional territory, from being expected to remain excellent, and from having no language of complaint that would fit her self-image.

This is why the book’s “monster” is simultaneously ordinary and terrifying. Stress is not dramatic enough for tragedy, but it is persistent enough to deform the self.

The sawarineko drains energy from others because Hanekawa has no permitted way to release her own exhaustion. It externalizes the invisible household economy of emotional depletion: she has been drained by her home, so the cat drains the town.

That is a very elegant supernatural mechanism.

It also reframes *Tsubasa Cat* in *Bakemonogatari*. The later outbreak is not a new problem. It is a recurrence. The stress has changed object — by then it includes Araragi, Senjougahara, jealousy, romantic loss, and the impossibility of remaining the perfect friend — but the underlying system is the same.

Hanekawa cannot process pain as herself, so something else processes it for her.

---

## 6\. Araragi’s love, denial, and the Tsukihi conversation

One of the most important early sections is Araragi asking Tsukihi whether he is in love with Hanekawa.

On the surface, the scene is buried in obscene sibling banter, underwear jokes, and Araragi’s usual verbal clowning. Structurally, though, it is one of the book’s key diagnostic scenes. Araragi has just come out of *Kizumonogatari*. He has rejected his old “human strength” philosophy, but he has almost no emotional literacy. He does not know what to call his own feeling.

Tsukihi’s answer is simple and sharp: if he is asking whether it is love, that itself is already suspiciously close to love. Reasons are often post-hoc constructions. You do not determine love by measuring the exact line where ordinary interest becomes affection.

That is important because Araragi keeps trying to rationalize Hanekawa. He thinks about her body, her kindness, her heroism, her role in spring break, her intelligence, her beauty, her status as savior. He wants to know which reason explains the feeling.

Tsukihi’s point is that this is backwards. The feeling comes first; the reasons are scaffolding.

This scene also deepens Araragi’s later relationship with Senjougahara. His feeling for Hanekawa is intense, but inchoate, indebted, idealizing, and tangled with *Kizu*. He does not fully know whether it is love, desire, gratitude, worship, guilt, or rescue. It is probably all of those at once.

That is why he cannot become Hanekawa’s romantic answer. He is too entangled in her image as the person who saved him.

---

## 7\. Araragi’s misrecognition of Hanekawa

The great Araragi failure in *Neko Black* is not that he does nothing. He does a lot. He investigates, worries, confronts, schemes, sacrifices his body, borrows the Kokorowatari, and almost dies.

His failure is that he still misreads Hanekawa.

He thinks, even after seeing the bruise and hearing her family story, that Hanekawa is “Hanekawa,” therefore she will somehow be all right. He knows the facts but does not absorb their meaning. He treats her competence as evidence of resilience rather than evidence of over-adaptation.

This is a subtle but serious error.

He does not ignore her suffering. He sees it. But he keeps interpreting it through the prior Hanekawa image:

> Hanekawa is smart.
> Hanekawa is strong.
> Hanekawa knows what she is doing.
> Hanekawa can handle things.

That image is exactly the prison.

The later house scene breaks him because it destroys that comforting interpretation. He finally sees the scale of the absence around her. Not just “Hanekawa has a bad home life,” but “Hanekawa has been living as if she were not really resident in her own home.”

That is when his earlier idealization becomes morally inadequate.

---

## 8\. “I won’t pity you”: Araragi’s harshest compassion

The climax contains one of Araragi’s most important ethical moves. He refuses to pity Hanekawa.

This is easy to misread as cruelty. It is cruelty, in form. He tells her that her life is terrible, that her past will not be erased, that future happiness will not cancel what happened, that she will carry it forever. He refuses consolation. He refuses the neat healing narrative.

But his refusal of pity is also the only language that can reach Hanekawa and the cat.

Pity would place Hanekawa below him.
Pity would make her a victim-object.
Pity would repeat the very hierarchy that Hanekawa’s strange kindness refuses.

Araragi understands, partly because of *Kizu*, that Hanekawa’s greatness was not that she “felt sorry” for him. She did not make him pitiable. She treated him as a person, even when he was a quasi-vampire disaster. He now tries, clumsily and violently, to return the same recognition.

So his message is not “your suffering does not matter.”

It is closer to:

> Your suffering is real, permanent, and unfair.
> But it does not give you permission to stop being yourself.
> Do not hide behind the cat.
> Do not let tragedy become an identity that exempts you from living.

That is an astonishingly harsh form of care. It is also very Araragi: morally sincere, psychologically perceptive, and dangerously self-sacrificial.

He is right enough to hurt her.

He is not right enough to save her.

---

## 9\. “You can be unhappy and still keep living”

The book’s most mature idea is that suffering does not need to become narratively redemptive.

Araragi says, in effect, that even if Hanekawa becomes happy later, the bad past will not vanish. It will remain bad. She will remember it. She will dream it. It will accumulate.

That is a major departure from simplistic trauma narratives. *Neko Black* does not offer:

> Once you confront the pain, you are healed.

Nor:

> The past made you stronger, so it was worth it.

Nor:

> The monster represents trauma, and exorcising the monster solves trauma.

Instead, it says something more adult and more brutal:

> The past remains.
> The household remains.
> The wound remains.
> The best available outcome may be postponement, containment, and the chance to continue.

This fits the afterword’s idea that not all problems are solved. Some become part of the environment. That does not mean they are good. It means life often consists of carrying unresolved wrongness without letting it become the whole self.

That is a very different ethic from heroic salvation.

---

## 10\. Kokorowatari as false solution

The妖刀「心渡」 / Kokorowatari seems, at first, like the perfect tool. It kills only oddities. Therefore, Araragi can separate the sawarineko from Hanekawa without harming Hanekawa.

This looks like the fantasy of clean intervention:

> Cut out the monster.
> Leave the person intact.
> End the story.

But *Neko Black* exposes this as a cheat.

The sawarineko is not the core problem. It is the form the problem has taken. Cutting out the cat does not solve Hanekawa’s home, stress, repression, or lack of belonging. It returns the system to the state before the outbreak. That is not healing. That is a reset.

Araragi knows this. His inner narration admits that defeating the cat completes the “story” but does not solve the “problem.”

That distinction may be the most important literary idea in the book:

> A story can end before the problem is solved.

In fact, stories often do this. They create a dramatic event, resolve the event, and leave the underlying life intact. *Neko Black* is unusually honest about that. It calls its own climax a compromise, a reset, a postponement.

This also explains why Oshino does not simply solve the case with brute force. As a balancer, he understands that deleting the oddity is not the same as restoring balance. Araragi, being Araragi, chooses the self-destructive shortcut anyway.

---

## 11\. Araragi’s self-sacrifice reaches its most grotesque form

Araragi’s plan with Kokorowatari is one of his most disturbing acts so far.

He hides the sword inside his own body, allows Black Hanekawa to attack him, and uses his body as a trap. This is technically clever, but psychologically horrifying. It takes the Araragi method — “I will use my body as payment” — to an absurd extreme.

After *Kizu*, this makes sense. Araragi learned that his body could become a site of exchange: blood, wounds, healing, vampirism, sacrifice. After *Bake*, he repeatedly uses pain as proof of commitment. In *Neko Black*, he turns his own body into equipment.

This is why Araragi is not simply a good-hearted helper. His ethics are bodily self-violence. He would rather be mutilated than admit helplessness.

Even worse, he frames dying for Hanekawa as happiness. That is not romantic nobility. It is dangerous self-erasure. The novel knows this; it lets him recognize his own self-satisfaction and self-intoxication, but recognition does not stop him.

Araragi wants to die for Hanekawa partly because it would give his feeling a form.

He cannot be her lover.
He cannot fix her family.
He cannot make her ask for help.
He cannot undo the past.

But he can die.

That is why Shinobu’s intervention matters so much. She interrupts not only his death but his fantasy of meaningful self-sacrifice.

---

## 12\. Shinobu’s intervention: you do not get to die alone

Chronologically, this is before the fuller speaking partnership of *Nisemonogatari*, but structurally it is a major turning point in Araragi and Shinobu’s relationship.

Shinobu appears because Araragi has been feeding her too much blood. That is partly comic plot mechanics, but symbolically it is perfect. He has been sustaining the person he condemned to live in *Kizu*, perhaps out of guilt, perhaps out of habit, perhaps out of attachment. That accumulated blood gives her enough power to intervene.

She then heals him and handles the cat properly. Her implicit rebuke is devastating: Araragi cannot simply keep her alive and then decide to die whenever his self-sacrificial mood demands it.

This changes the ethical field.

In *Kizu*, Araragi refused to let Kiss-shot die.
In *Neko Black*, Shinobu refuses to let Araragi die.

That reciprocity matters. Their bond is no longer only guilt flowing one way. It is mutual interference. Each has lost the right to vanish cleanly.

Her absorption of the sawarineko/stress also reconfigures the solution. Araragi’s blade-separation is crude and destabilizing. Shinobu’s vampiric devouring actually removes the immediate supernatural excess. But even that still does not solve Hanekawa’s life. It only prevents catastrophe.

Shinobu’s role here is not “the real savior.” She is the one who prevents Araragi’s bad solution from becoming fatal.

---

## 13\. Black Hanekawa’s speech: cat language as liberated obscenity

Black Hanekawa’s “にゃ” speech is easy to treat as a fetish-comedy device, and it is partly that. But it also has psychological significance.

Hanekawa’s ordinary speech is controlled, correct, polite, composed, rational. Black Hanekawa’s speech is playful, crude, erotic, predatory, teasing, and physically immediate. The cat grammar breaks the class-president grammar.

It lets Hanekawa say things she cannot say as Hanekawa.

But this does not mean Black Hanekawa is “the true Hanekawa.” That would be too easy. Black Hanekawa is also a distortion. She is not truth in pure form; she is repressed pressure expressed through an oddity’s style.

So the opposition is not:

> Hanekawa = fake
> Black Hanekawa = real

It is:

> Hanekawa = overcontrolled self
> Black Hanekawa = uncontrolled outlet
> neither alone = whole person

That is the real tragedy. Hanekawa has no integrated self that can say, calmly and humanly: “I am hurt. I am angry. I need help. I hate this house. I want to be loved. I want Araragi. I am not okay.”

Because she cannot say those things, the cat says them in mangled form.

---

## 14\. “I only know what I know” becomes newly important

The famous Hanekawa phrase — she does not know everything, only what she knows — gains its real force here.

Araragi uses Kokorowatari partly because he has one piece of knowledge Hanekawa does not. This is crucial. Hanekawa is terrifying because she sees through almost every plan. Oshino cannot beat the sawarineko not merely because of raw power, but because Hanekawa’s intelligence gives the oddity strategic advantage.

Araragi wins only because there is something she does not know: the hidden property and availability of Kokorowatari.

That victory is symbolically perfect. Hanekawa’s despair partly rests on a false omniscience: she has assessed her life, her household, her suffering, her future, and concluded that there is no meaningful way out. Araragi’s counter is not to offer a real solution. He proves only that she does not know everything.

That matters.

If she does not know everything, then she cannot close the book on herself. She cannot say “死んじゃえ” to herself as final verdict. She cannot treat her present misery as total knowledge of future life.

Araragi’s message is basically:

> You do not know enough to condemn yourself.

That is one of the most powerful things he says in the early series.

It does not solve her pain. But it blocks the absolute form of despair.

---

## 15\. Why Araragi is not Hanekawa’s hero

Black Hanekawa tells Araragi he can become a star but not a hero. Araragi replies that he cannot even become a star; he can only become a vampire, and even that he failed at.

This exchange is central.

Araragi wants to help, but he is not a hero in the clean sense. He does not rescue Hanekawa from her life. He does not defeat domestic neglect. He does not give her a home. He does not return her suffering to ordinary proportion. He cannot even complete his own self-sacrificial plan without Shinobu saving him.

That failure is not a writing flaw. It is the point.

Araragi’s role in Hanekawa’s life is powerful but insufficient. He can interrupt despair. He can refuse pity. He can risk himself. He can reveal that she does not know everything. But he cannot be the solution to Hanekawa.

This will matter profoundly later. Hanekawa’s growth cannot be completed through Araragi loving her, choosing her, saving her, or dying for her. In fact, Araragi’s idealization is part of the problem. He sees too much “Hanekawa” and not enough Tsubasa.

*Neko Black* begins the dismantling of Araragi-as-Hanekawa’s-hero. *Tsubasa Cat* continues it. Later material will have to complete it.

---

## 16\. Pity, equality, and “かわいそうじゃない”

The late realization that Hanekawa did not pity the dead cat, and did not pity Araragi in *Kizu*, is one of the deepest insights in the book.

Araragi recognizes that being treated as **かわいそう** can become a form of being looked down on. Hanekawa’s strange virtue is that she does not reduce wounded beings to pitiable objects. She treats them as equal existences.

That is why the cat loved her, in its own way.

That is why Araragi loved or almost-loved her.

This gives Hanekawa’s compassion a very specific quality. It is not sentimental warmth. It is a refusal to hierarchize suffering.

But again, the virtue has a cost. Because Hanekawa does not pity others, she also does not pity herself. Because she refuses to dramatize suffering, she cannot easily ask for rescue. Her equality becomes self-erasure.

The book’s ethical insight is therefore double-edged:

> Pity can be condescending.
> But a total refusal of pity can leave suffering with nowhere human to go.

Hanekawa needs something beyond pity and beyond stoic correctness. She needs recognition that does not look down on her but still allows her to need help.

Araragi can gesture toward that. He cannot yet provide it.

---

## 17\. The “black” in *Neko Black*

The black/white symbolism is not just visual. It relates to front/back, surface/reverse, innocence/violence, correctness/repression.

The sawarineko begins with a white cat, and Black Hanekawa has white hair. So “black” is not simply literal color. It is the black side of the family story, the hidden underside of the white class-president image, the reverse side of “Tsubasa.”

It is also the first half of a larger Hanekawa structure. If *Neko Black* is the repressed underside erupting through the cat, then later *Neko White* will have to address something more integrated: not just stress being offloaded, but Hanekawa herself confronting the split.

For now, black means:

> the side of Hanekawa that the perfect image cannot include.

But the novel is careful not to treat that side as pure truth. It is not enough for the black side to emerge. Emergence without integration becomes catastrophe.

---

## 18\. The ending: “problem postponed,” not problem solved

The final emotional beat is harshly funny and very important.

Araragi says something like: we are all terrible, unhappy, unrewarded, and unable to fix the past, but maybe that is okay.

Hanekawa’s unconscious reply is essentially: no, it is not okay.

She is right.

This is the correct ending.

If Hanekawa had accepted Araragi’s grim optimism, the novel would become dishonest. Her problem is not okay. Her family situation is not okay. Her stress is not okay. The cat incident is not okay. Araragi’s self-mutilation is not okay. Shinobu’s emergency intervention is not okay.

But they live.

So the novel ends in the only honest way available:

> not resolution, but postponement.

This is why *Neko Black* is so important to the larger series. It refuses to let the reader believe that supernatural climax equals psychological healing. It leaves Hanekawa’s life intact in the worst sense: still there, still waiting, still unsolved.

That unresolvedness becomes the engine for later Hanekawa arcs.

---

# How *Neko Black* revises our previous readings

## It revises *Kizumonogatari*

In *Kizu*, Hanekawa appears almost impossibly brave, kind, and humanizing. *Neko Black* shows that this was real — but not healthy in the simple sense. Her ability to treat Araragi without pity came from the same inner structure that prevents her from treating herself as someone who deserves care.

Her virtue and pathology are intertwined.

## It revises *Bakemonogatari*

*Tsubasa Cat* is no longer Hanekawa’s first breakdown. It is a recurrence after a suppressed first outbreak. That means her jealousy over Araragi/Senjougahara is not a standalone romantic wound. It is a new stressor added to a preexisting system of repression.

The cat was not “about Araragi” first. It was about family. Later, Araragi becomes part of the stress structure.

## It revises *Nisemonogatari*

The fake/real problem from *Nise* also echoes backward. Hanekawa’s family is real in the legal sense and false in the emotional sense. Araragi’s family is grotesque in presentation but real in attachment. A “real” family can fail to function as family; a ridiculous household can still produce belonging.

## It revises Araragi

Araragi’s self-sacrifice becomes even more suspect. He is not just brave. He is addicted to using his own body as an answer when no answer exists. *Neko Black* makes the problem painfully visible: his willingness to die for Hanekawa is meaningful, but also useless, self-indulgent, and interrupted only because Shinobu refuses to let him vanish.

---

# Final thesis

*Nekomonogatari Black* is the first great Hanekawa tragedy because it shows that Hanekawa’s problem is not ignorance, weakness, or hidden evil. Her problem is that she has converted an unlivable life into livable correctness.

The sawarineko does not create her darkness. It gives her stress claws, teeth, and an alibi.

Araragi does not save her. Oshino does not solve her. Shinobu prevents catastrophe. The family remains. The past remains. The stress is absorbed, not healed. The story ends, but the problem does not.

The most concise formulation:

> **『猫物語（黒）』 argues that some monsters are not the cause of suffering but the form suffering takes when a person is too competent, too kind, and too “correct” to admit she is being destroyed.**

That makes Hanekawa one of the most psychologically important characters in the entire early series. Senjougahara’s crab removed her weight. Araragi’s vampire wound created his rescue compulsion. Karen and Tsukihi revealed the value and danger of fakes.

Hanekawa’s cat reveals something even more severe:

> A person can be so good at surviving that survival itself becomes the illness.

---
